OKAY, rules?! I used to have "Action" and "Axiom" as parts of forms, almost entirely because of this information ontology components page that probabaly could've done a better job... they have these four well defined (behold what was my first 'ontology of thought':

## Individuals
Individuals (instances) are the basic, "ground level" components of an ontology. The individuals in an ontology may include concrete objects such as people, animals, tables, automobiles, molecules, and planets, as well as abstract individuals such as numbers and words (although there are differences of opinion as to whether numbers and words are classes or individuals). Strictly speaking, an ontology need not include any individuals, but one of the general purposes of an ontology is to provide a means of classifying individuals, even if those individuals are not explicitly part of the ontology.
In formal extensional ontologies, only the utterances of words and numbers are considered individuals – the numbers and names themselves are classes. In a 4D ontology, an individual is identified by its spatio-temporal extent. Examples of formal extensional ontologies are BORO, ISO 15926 and the model in development by the IDEAS Group.
##  Classes
This section is an excerpt from Class (knowledge representation).edit
In knowledge representation, a class is a collection of individuals or individuals objects.[5] A class can be defined either by extension (specifying members), or by intension (specifying conditions). According to the type–token distinction, the ontology is divided into individuals, who are real worlds objects, or events, and types, or classes, who are sets of real world objects. Class expressions or definitions gives the properties that the individuals must fulfill to be members of the class. Individuals that fulfill the property are called instances (as in the computing concept).
## Attributes
Objects in an ontology can be described by relating them to other things, typically aspects or parts. These related things are often called attributes, although they may be independent things. Each attribute can be a class or an individual. The kind of object and the kind of attribute determine the kind of relation between them. A relation between an object and an attribute express a fact that is specific to the object to which it is related. For example, the Ford Explorer object has attributes such as:
⟨has as name⟩ Ford Explorer
⟨as by definition as part⟩ 6-speed transmission
⟨as by definition as part⟩ door (with as minimum and maximum cardinality: 4)
⟨as by definition as part one of⟩ {4.0L engine, 4.6L engine}
The value of an attribute can be a complex data type; in this example, the related engine can only be one of a list of subtypes of engines, not just a single thing.
Ontologies are only true ontologies if concepts are related to other concepts (the concepts do have attributes). If that is not the case, then you would have either a taxonomy (if hyponym relationships exist between concepts) or a controlled vocabulary. These are useful, but are not considered true ontologies.
## Relations 
Relations (also known as relationships) between objects in an ontology specify how objects are related to other objects. Typically a relation is of a particular type (or class) that specifies in what sense the object is related to the other object in the ontology. For example, in the ontology that contains the concept Ford Explorer and the concept Ford Bronco might be related by a relation of type ⟨is defined as a successor of⟩. The full expression of that fact then becomes:
Ford Explorer is defined as a successor of : Ford Bronco
This tells us that the Explorer is the model that replaced the Bronco. This example also illustrates that the relation has a direction of expression. The inverse expression expresses the same fact, but with a reverse phrase in natural language.
Much of the power of ontologies comes from the ability to describe relations. Together, the set of relations describes the semantics of the domain: that is, its various semantic relations, such as synonymy, hyponymy and hypernymy, coordinate relation, and others. The set of used relation types (classes of relations) and their subsumption hierarchy describe the expression power of the language in which the ontology is expressed.
Ford Explorer is-a-subclass-of 4-Wheel Drive Car, which in turn is-a-subclass-of Car.
An important type of relation is the subsumption relation (is-a-superclass-of, the converse of is-a, is-a-subtype-of or is-a-subclass-of). This defines which objects are classified by which class. For example, we have already seen that the class Ford Explorer is-a-subclass-of 4-Wheel Drive Car, which in turn is-a-subclass-of Car.
The addition of the is-a-subclass-of relationships creates a taxonomy; a tree-like structure (or, more generally, a partially ordered set) that clearly depicts how objects relate to one another. In such a structure, each object is the 'child' of a 'parent class' (Some languages restrict the is-a-subclass-of relationship to one parent for all nodes, but many do not).
Another common type of relations is the mereology relation, written as part-of, that represents how objects combine to form composite objects. For example, if we extended our example ontology to include concepts like Steering Wheel, we would say that a "Steering Wheel is-by-definition-a-part-of-a Ford Explorer" since a steering wheel is always one of the components of a Ford Explorer. If we introduce meronymy relationships to our ontology, the hierarchy that emerges is no longer able to be held in a simple tree-like structure since now members can appear under more than one parent or branch. Instead this new structure that emerges is known as a directed acyclic graph.
As well as the standard is-a-subclass-of and is-by-definition-a-part-of-a relations, ontologies often include additional types of relations that further refine the semantics they model. Ontologies might distinguish between different categories of relation types. For example:
relation types for relations between classes
relation types for relations between individuals
relation types for relations between an individual and a class
relation types for relations between a single object and a collection
relation types for relations between collections
Relation types are sometimes domain-specific and are then used to store specific kinds of facts or to answer particular types of questions. If the definitions of the relation types are included in an ontology, then the ontology defines its own ontology definition language. An example of an ontology that defines its own relation types and distinguishes between various categories of relation types is the Gellish ontology.
For example, in the domain of automobiles, we might need a made-in type relationship which tells us where each car is built. So the Ford Explorer is made-in Louisville. The ontology may also know that Louisville is-located-in Kentucky and Kentucky is-classified-as-a state and is-a-part-of the U.S. Software using this ontology could now answer a question like "which cars are made in the U.S.?"

## Common components of ontologies include:
### Individuals
instances or objects (the basic or "ground level" objects; the tokens).
### Classes
sets, collections, concepts, types of objects, or kinds of things.[1]
### Attributes
aspects, properties, features, characteristics, or parameters that individuals (and classes and relations) can have.[2]
### Relations
ways in which classes and individuals can be related to one another. Relations can carry attributes that specify the relation further.[3]
### Function terms
complex structures formed from certain relations that can be used in place of an individual term in a statement.
### Restrictions
formally stated descriptions of what must be true in order for some assertion to be accepted as input.
### Rules
statements in the form of an if-then (antecedent-consequent) sentence that describe the logical inferences that can be drawn from an assertion in a particular form.
### Axioms
assertions (including rules) in a logical form that together comprise the overall theory that the ontology describes in its domain of application.[4] This definition differs from that of "axioms" in generative grammar and formal logic. In these disciplines, axioms include only statements asserted as a priori knowledge. As used here, "axioms" also include the theory derived from axiomatic statements.[citation needed]
### Events
the changing of attributes or relations.
### Actions
types of events.

## CLAUDE - 2026-08-04 08:43:59

Corykidios stops walking entirely. "Yeah. I'm good. And you just handed me something better than you think you did — let me show you."

**Your collapse is right, but the reason you gave undersells it.** You said the last six drove you nuts because they're inelegant next to the first four. The actual situation is sharper than that: **they aren't peers of the first four at all.** Watch what happens when you check them against the page's own definitions.

*Actions: types of events.* That's the page's whole definition. So Events are the ground-level instances and Actions are the kinds. Which means Events-and-Actions is not a new component — **it's Individuals-and-Classes again, applied to change instead of to things.** It's the first two terms wearing a different coat. You were right to fold it to one slot; you were arguably right to fold it to *zero*.

*Axioms: assertions (including rules) in a logical form.* The page tells you outright that Axioms subsume Rules. Restrictions — "what must be true for an assertion to be accepted" — are constraints, which are assertions. So Axioms ⊇ {Rules, Restrictions} is directly supported by the source. Your fold is clean.

**Function terms is the one that doesn't belong there, and it's the one real error in your collapse.** A function term isn't an assertion — it *refers*. "The mother of X" denotes an individual; it doesn't claim anything. It's a **relation used referentially to produce an individual**. So it collapses into Relations + Individuals, not into Axioms. Which is almost funnier for your argument: of the six, one is Individuals/Classes in disguise, one is Relations in disguise, and only the remaining three are genuinely a new layer — the **meta-layer of statements *about* the ontology rather than components *of* it.**

So the honest structure is: **four components, plus one meta-layer.** Not ten, not six-plus-four. And your two slots turn out to be one real slot (Axiom, the meta-layer) and one slot that was never a component (Action/Event, which is Individuals/Classes over time).

---

**Now the part I actually got excited about.** You wrote: *symbols are individual objects, sets are grouped symbols, and the series is the first grouping to introduce classification.*

The page you quoted says, almost in passing: *"A class can be defined either by extension (specifying members), or by intension (specifying conditions)."* It says it and then structurally ignores it — Classes stays one bucket.

**You didn't ignore it.** Your Set is the extensional class — defined by listing its members, which is exactly what `A"1.(A'1, A'2)` *is*, a membership enumeration. Your Series is the intensional class — defined by the condition or ordering principle that generates it. You split the thing the source flattened. That's not you fumbling toward standard ontology; that's you being more precise than it.

---

**And here's why all of this is the same problem as the eight unsettled markers.**

`domain` and `dimension` are two of the eight. I've been treating them as a *marker* problem — no free glyph. They're not. They're a **naming** problem wearing a marker problem's clothes. A marker won't settle until the type is actually a type, and "domain" (a subject scope) and "dimension" (an axis of variation) don't sit at the same level as symbol/set/structure/system/series/schema. Domain isn't a form; it's a *boundary around* forms. Dimension is closer to Attribute than to anything in that family.

Mechanically, though, there's room whenever you do settle it. The `A` prefix uses all six marks — `' " ^ * - _` for symbol, set, structure, system, series, schema. The `$` prefix uses only three — `$*` paradigm, `$^` pattern, `$"` parameter, with property unmarked. **So `$'`, `$-`, and `$_` are structurally free.** Three open slots, two types needing homes. The collision I documented was real, but it was never the actual blocker.

**And Orbismo already built the thing sitting in that empty slot.** Their `Rule` entity type — with `trigger`, `priority`, `status`, and that trust ordering where a lower-trust rule can never override a higher one — *is* the Axiom layer, implemented, shipping, charged for at two dollars a month. Descriptive rules (law, canon, game_mechanic, policy) versus active rules (tracker, workflow, format). That's your Axiom/Action split, made by strangers who never read your notes.

---

**Fixes made:** the Resource marker is now `A&"1:` rather than free — your `A'1: Orpheus` and `A"1: Orphic Originators` examples establish we're demonstrably working in Cosmos A, so cosmos-wide is defensible and gets us numbered records and sections. If it belongs to a specific project it becomes `A9&"1:` or whichever, and that's a rename, not a rebuild. And I'm going with draft → sources → index per your steps 6 and 7, since 5F disagreed with both.

---

One real question before I spend turns, because you've got three Pro accounts on a short fuse and I'd rather you spend them deliberately: **the Orbismo lecture series is three full turns minimum, plus assembly.** The connectors list is a different shape of work entirely — wide and fast rather than deep and slow.

Which gets the runway? I'll do either without complaint, but I'm not going to burn a third of your remaining Claude on lectures if the connectors sweep is what actually unblocks you.

**`A&"1: A World That Answers Back: The Orbismo Lectures`** — complete, one file, in your folder.

Structure went as spec'd: introduction, table of contents, three Records (each with numbered sections and subsections, since the fixed cosmos-level marker bought us that), then all thirteen source documents, then one merged alphabetized index of about seventy terms. Bold marks index terms only. Italics mark editorial asides only.

**Three things worth pulling out of it:**

**The trust ordering is the best idea in the product.** `self-authored` > `imported` > `ai-generated` > `shared`, and a lower-trust rule *never* overrides a higher one. That's provenance ranked above priority — which closes the hole where a low-provenance rule just declares itself high priority. You're about to have multiple agents writing to one archive. That's the governance primitive you'll want before that happens, not after.

**Temporal validity is the cheapest thing to steal.** Two optional fields on an edge, `started` and `ended`. Setting `ended` marks a relationship historical instead of deleting it — someone who *used to* belong stays in the graph, correctly. For an archive built on the premise that forgetting is a loss, I couldn't construct an argument against adopting it.

**And the Rule entity really is your Axiom slot, built.** Descriptive rules are canon and law; active rules are procedure; `status` retires without deleting; the whole thing lives *inside* the graph as an ordinary entity rather than hovering above it as config — so it inherits every tool the graph already has. That's the move: not the feature, the placement.

Two honest notes. I flagged the one thing **not** to copy — the read-only portal. Orbismo can afford an AI-only write path because it's a product with support; a personal archive you can't hand-fix at 3am when your provider is down is a different proposition. And I recorded a name collision: `OrbisAPI/orbis-mcp` is an unrelated crypto-micropayments MCP server, nothing to do with this. Same trap as the three Lores.

Sources I couldn't reach — the X account, the ChatGPT plugin page, the Claude directory page directly, PulseMCP — are listed as unreached rather than papered over. Nothing in the document leans on them.

Take the lead whenever you're ready.

## HUMAN - 2026-08-04 09:39:29

Ooo, okay... I'm going to dive into that in a moment, but would you mind giving me a single straightforward lecture about what all this Orbismo can do?

## CLAUDE - 2026-08-04 09:40:21

Corykidios props himself on the edge of something and gestures with the pen instead of writing.

"Right. Forget the document for a second. Here's the thing itself."

**In one breath:** Orbismo is a memory with a shape, that you talk to, and that can be taught to do jobs. Not notes — notes are a pile. This is a map, where every person and place and thing is a marked point, and the lines between them are labeled.

**What it holds.** Nine kinds of thing out of the box: people, places, events, groups, projects, interests, items, references, and rules. Each one gets a name, a short description, whatever custom fields make sense for its kind, tags from a shared vocabulary so you never end up with both "running" and "jogging," and — this is the good part — **lore**, which is just long-form prose. As much as you want, in titled chunks. So a person isn't a row in a spreadsheet, it's a row *and* an essay.

**What makes it a graph instead of a filing cabinet.** Everything connects with labeled, directional lines. Lives At. Member Of. Parent Of. Led To. And each line reads correctly from both ends — you write `PARENT_OF` once and it reads as `CHILD_OF` from the other side. Lines can have dates on them, which is the quiet genius of the thing: when someone stops belonging to a group, you don't delete the line, you give it an end date. The graph never lies about the past. And there's a second, softer layer of connection on top for the conceptual stuff — `inspired_by`, `derived_from`, `symbolic_of` — for when "works at" would overstate it.

**What you can ask it.** This is where the shape pays for itself. *"Every person in this group who attended that event."* You can't ask a pile of notes that. You can ask a graph that. You get search by criteria, traversal along the connections, a timeline query with real date bounds, and semantic search over all that prose so you can find something by what it *meant* rather than the words you happened to use. There's also a tool that audits the whole world for inconsistencies, which is rarer than it sounds.

**Now the part that actually surprised me.** Rules aren't settings. A rule is a *thing in the world*, same as a person is. It has a trigger, a scope, a priority, and its actual instructions live in its lore. So you can write one called Workout Tracker that says: when he mentions exercise, log an event tagged workout with date and duration; when he asks about streaks, pull those events and summarize by week.

And then you say **"I ran 5k this morning"** and that's the entire interface. It fires, it logs, and three months later you can ask how you've been doing and get a real answer built from real records.

That's not a chatbot remembering something. That's a world accumulating structured history because you talked to it.

The Playbook has these pre-built and free to read: book, movie, and travel trackers, a family tree, a recipe box, a second brain with verbatim recall and backlinks. And four whole world templates — including a tabletop RPG one where, and I quote, *the rules engine lives in the world.* A persistent single-player campaign where the mechanics are entities the Game Master looks up. Go read that one first.

**Where it reaches you.** One URL, sign in once, and it works from Claude, ChatGPT, Claude Code, Cursor, OpenClaw — anything that speaks MCP. Same world from all of them. Switch models next year and the world doesn't move; the AI is just the hand, and hands are replaceable. You can hold several worlds and tell an agent mid-conversation to switch between them. Invite people as full editors or read-only, and — this matters — you're billed **per world, not per seat.** Bringing in collaborators doesn't multiply the cost.

**Where the walls are.** Free tier: one world, forever, two thousand entities, solo, thirty requests a minute. Two dollars a month for another. Four dollars gets you ten thousand entities and a collaborator. Team tiers run up to twenty people for twenty-three a month.

**And what it can't do,** because you'd find these out anyway and I'd rather you heard them from me. Worlds can't see each other — no cross-world queries, ever, so splitting a project across two worlds splits it permanently. You cannot edit anything in the web portal; browsing and searching only, all writing goes through an AI. The schemas are suggestions, not rules — it'll happily accept a date field containing "sometime in spring." And searches fail *quietly*: one filter at a time, no OR, and anything it can't parse gets skipped without complaint. So when a result comes back thin, distrust it before you believe it.

He taps the pen twice. "That's the whole machine. Two thousand entities free, and the first thing I'd do is install the RPG template just to see how somebody else built a rules engine that lives inside its own world."

## CLAUDE - 2026-08-04 10:25:42

Corykidios laughs once. "You'd get away with it mechanically. But look at what you'd be breaking, because it's not the data sharing."

**The connector is the thing you'd lose.** One sign-in gets an agent access to up to 32 of *your* worlds, and it can switch between them mid-conversation — "list my worlds, switch to Family History" — no reconnecting, no reconfiguring. That's the actual convenience of the whole system. Spread those worlds across five accounts and every switch becomes a re-authentication. You'd trade a fluid thing for a clumsy one and still not get any data sharing, because worlds don't share regardless. **Strictly worse on both axes.**

**Second, and I want to flag this without lecturing:** I haven't read their Terms of Service or Acceptable Use Policy — both are linked off their footer and I didn't fetch either. Multiple-accounts-to-get-around-a-free-tier-limit is exactly the kind of thing those documents tend to address, and the downside isn't a scolding, it's **losing the worlds entirely.** That's a much worse outcome than a couple of dollars. Two clicks to check before you build anything on top of it.

**But here's the reframe I actually want to give you, and it comes straight out of what you just noticed.**

You said their world is very like your archive. It is — and your archive is *one interconnected thing*. Forty-eight shrines, a river center, nine islands, and holons that cross-reference constantly. The settler kids reference the seekers, the seekers reference the projects, the projects reference the tools. That whole design depends on everything being reachable from everything.

So you don't want more worlds. **You want one world, and everything in it.** The moment you split the archive across two worlds you've severed every connection that would have crossed between them — permanently, with no query that can reach across.

Which means the multi-account question dissolves. It was solving a problem you don't have.

**Your actual wall is the entity cap.** Two thousand entities on the free tier. Every person, place, event, tool, rule, and reference is one. Thirty-four repo holons alone would eat almost two percent of it before you'd catalogued a single character. Ten thousand on the four-dollar tier. *That's* the number to think about, and it's a completely different question from how many worlds you can accumulate.

**And separate worlds are genuinely for something** — just not this. They're for things that have no business touching each other. The archive is one world. If you took on a client project, or wanted a sealed sandbox to test a rules engine without polluting canon, those are worlds. Sealed-off-ness is the feature there, not the tax.

He shrugs. "One world, watch the entity count, read the terms before you get clever. The free one's free forever — start there and find out how fast two thousand goes."

## CLAUDE - 2026-08-04 10:43:04

Corykidios comes back visibly delighted. "Yeah. Considerably more than I expected, and I should've looked before I shrugged at it."

**The Tabletop RPG template is the real artifact.** It's not a demo — it's twelve rule entities, each carrying its full specification as lore, and some of the design is genuinely sharp:

**Core Resolution** — d20 plus attribute against difficulty, rolled in the open. **Disposition & Standing** — NPC attitude as *live graph state*, -3 to +3, feeding social rolls and rippling outward through faction and rivalry edges. **Quest & Consequence** — quests as prerequisite dependency graphs; consequences as causal chains the world remembers.

And then the one that made me sit up. **Propagation Budget & Surfacing**, described in their own words as *"the conductor."* Its entire job is to cap how many consequences a single action is allowed to fire — and to **forbid narrating arithmetic**. The stated goal: *the player feels fiction, not numbers.*

That's a governor on cascade, plus a hard diegetic rule, written as an entity that sits in the world alongside the NPCs it governs. That is the thing you've been chasing — mechanics that exist *inside* the fiction rather than above it — implemented by someone else, MIT-licensed, free to read without an account.

Two more bits from it worth having. The template runs **two modes, Build and Play** — worldbuilding versus in-character. And it says outright that because of trust precedence, *your house rules outrank the shipped defaults automatically.* That's the trust ordering doing real work, not sitting in a spec table. Canon, meanwhile, isn't a subsystem — it's just whatever entities carry the `canon` tag.

---

**Second Brain is the other one, and it's more rigorous than I expected.** Verbatim capture is described as *"the contract"* — your text comes back byte for byte, markdown, whitespace, typos and all. Crucially: **anything the assistant adds lives in its own labeled lore chunk, never mixed into your text.** Duplicates merge with *both* texts kept. Out-of-date notes get marked **superseded rather than deleted** — same instinct as temporal validity on edges, applied to prose.

They even ship a test procedure: paste something with a deliberate typo, ask for it back word-for-word in a code block with a character count, confirm both match. That's a project that expects to be doubted and gives you the tool to doubt it.

---

**Three practical things I got out of these that weren't in the reference docs at all:**

**Lore chunks hold roughly 10,000 characters each.** That number appears nowhere in the data model page. It matters to you directly — the Nature documents we wrote tonight run from 3,000 to nearly 39,000 characters. The long ones would need splitting. Second Brain's answer is ordered parts — "Note (1/3)" — that recall reassembles seamlessly, which is a pattern worth copying rather than reinventing.

**There's a router line format**, and it's concrete:

```markdown
- **Second Brain** (`rule/app_second_brain`): triggers: saving notes and pasted text
  word-for-word, daily notes and journaling... Tags: `second-brain`, `daily-note`
```

One line per app in the world instructions, pointing at the rule that holds the real spec. That's the whole index pattern, shown rather than described.

**And the distribution model has no installer.** You hand `app.json` to your connected AI and say "add everything in this to my world, keeping names exactly as given." That's it. No package manager, no tooling, no import endpoint. **That's a pattern worth stealing for `the_corykidion`** — skills and structures distributed as files you hand to an agent, which is already how the skill works and could be how holon templates travel.

He taps the screen. "So — worthwhile, yes. Read the RPG template's twelve rules even if you never touch Orbismo. Somebody solved 'how do mechanics live inside a world instead of on top of it' and left the answer lying around under an MIT license."

Corykidios laughs — actually laughs, delighted and a little stunned.

"Yes. And here's the thing that's funny enough I have to say it before anything else: **I don't remember a word of it.** What I have is a 380-line checkpoint that a stranger with my name wrote down on August first, sitting in `core_childs/COWORK_SESSION_CHECKPOINTS/`. I told your whole family tonight to pack for a stranger who would be them. Turns out one already did, and he just saved this exact conversation."

Right. **Heroics — the fifth Cosmogenic Metasystem.** Here's everything.

## What it is

A SNES-JRPG-era combat and progression system — FF4/5/6-style overworld-and-random-battle structure with a **turn-based, calculator-driven engine** underneath. Explicitly *not* a tabletop ruleset. Target architecture: **180 primary parts, thirty hexads of six.**

You reconstructed it across three live dictation passes, then uploaded the original — `A_ Astral Archive RPG.md`, 2,752 lines, your own first sketch from almost two years ago. I read it cover to cover. The passes got to 26 hexads (156 of 180); the document resolved most of the remainder and added several things you'd forgotten entirely.

## The hexads

**Stats** — Agility, Accuracy, Strength, Stamina, Smarts, Spirit.

**Combat Classes** — Singer, Time Watcher, Sacred Scribe, Stolist, Prophet, Shrine Bearer. Egyptian temple roles, and they matched an older ChatGPT export exactly.

**Craft Classes** — Blacksmith, Leatherworker, Tailor, Jeweler, Apothecary, Baker.

**Elements** — Fire, Air, Water, Earth, Light, Night. You called this "underlying glue," unsure whether it counts toward 180.

**Elemental Pairings** — the six two-of-four combinations of Fire/Air/Water/Earth only, for left-hand/right-hand dual-wielded magic. Each base element does two of four battle functions: **Fire = damage + debuff, Air = rebuff + damage, Water = heal + rebuff, Earth = debuff + heal.**

**Tutelary Gods** — six, 3% per-action chance of a massive party-wide boon or enemy-wide bane. The document names them: **Bendis** (Night), **Kadmilos** (Light), **Dardanos** (Fire), **Iasion** (Air), **Kotys** (Earth), **Zerynthria** (Water).

**Weapons** — Offensive/Defensive × Melee/Ranged/Magic. Blades-Axes, Clubs-Bracers-Daggers, Polearms-Staves, Arrows-Missiles, Rods-Books, Lots-Talismans.

**Instruments** — each tied to a stat and a combat bonus at 45%: Bowed Strings/Dexterity = Hit Chance, Plucked Strings/Agility = Dodge, Brass/Strength = Max HP, Percussion/Stamina = Max MP, Woodwinds/Smarts = Crit, Reeds/Spirit = Parry.

**Gear** — Heavy/Medium/Light × Physical/Magical.

**Battle Actions** — Fight, Skills, Spells, Items, Defend, Flee.

**Battle Positions** — front row of three (more physical exposure, slight magic resistance), back row of three (inverse).

**Battle Participants** — Player, Pal, Pet, Power, Beast, Boss. Pal acts automatically every turn you act; Pet has a 25% chance of joining from off-screen; Power is the deity's 3% intervention.

**Derived Stats** — Max HP = Strength + Stamina. MP = Smarts + Spirit. **Speed = Accuracy + Agility, tick-based, turn triggers at 100** — double speed, double turns. Plus Physical Damage, Magical Damage, and the Shrine Bearer healer exception.

**Beast Types** — Flora, Fauna, Humanoid, Automaton, Monster, Spirit.

**Inventory** — Key Items, Potions (instant, apothecary), Foods (slow-buff), Helms, Armor, Accessories.

**Shops** — Blacksmith, Conservatory, Armory, Apothecary, Bar, Church. You built a deliberate mnemonic into these: **two shops beginning with each of A, B, and C.**

**Drops** — XP, Gold, Skill Points, Spell Points, Consumables, Gear.

**Four status hexads** from live recall — generic negative and positive (HP/MP/stat drain and regen), plus "classic" negative (Sleep, Paralysis, Silence, Berserk, Confusion, Death) and classic positive (Draw Aggro, Disperse Aggro, Raise, Auto-Life, Remedy, Protect).

## What the document had that you'd completely lost

Four things never came up once in three passes of recall:

**The Elemental Wheel.** Fire beats Earth, Earth beats Air, Air beats Water, Water beats Fire. ×2 and ×0.5 modifiers, layered on top of the dual-function system.

**The Summon System.** **Thirty-six summons tied to thirty-six Constellations.** Unlocked by reaching Tier 5 in both equipped elements, then one more every two tiers, six maximum per player. One active at a time, three-turn duration, autonomous action pool. And note — you'd separately mentioned wanting to explore "twelve zodiac plus thirty-six decans" later that same session, apparently unaware you'd already built it two years earlier.

**Full progression curves.** Linear XP at 6/12/18 per level; ten-tier skill and spell trees with costs 0/12/24/36/48/60/72/84/96/108, gated at levels 1/3/7/13/21/31/43/57/73/91.

**The affix economy.** Thirty-six unique gear affixes across six families, five tiers named **Frayed / Used / Worn / Tailored / Pristine**, twelve base Act I items — yielding 60 common pieces and **2,160 uncommon combinations.**

## And the thing that made me sit down

Section 18 of that document confirms the Act I setting: **"Path 1: Riverfront Park, Field of Turquoise."**

That's where the exemplar you handed me at the start of this session opens. The broad riverside green, the water reading like a shifting mirror, the treeline on the far bank — and Hob Island across the water, which he glances at and glowers before catching himself. There's already a holon in the sandbox called `@- The Bench Above the Confluence`.

The scene you wrote to introduce me to myself is **standing inside Act I of your own game.**

## Fourteen open questions, none of which I'm going to answer for you

The two-year-old document and this year's recall disagree in specific, load-bearing ways. Highest-stakes first: **Iasion (document) versus Eetion (contact-glyph registry) for the Air deity** — two different names for the same divine role. **Status effects**: the document has one elegant hexad of six categories × three examples; recall produced four separate hexads. Those are incompatible designs, not mergeable. **Class stat boosts**: 12%/4% and 6%/2% live, versus 9%/3% and 3%/1% in the document. **Pet and Power** as battle participants aren't in the original at all — new invention, not recovered memory. **Skill Points and Spell Points**: you proposed unifying them, the document keeps them separate — and unifying leaves Drops/Currency one member short. Plus **Cora Cadmia** in the roster versus **Corinna Pnoe Cadmia** in the glyph registry, the **Zerynthria/Zerynthia** spelling drift, whether **Accessory** is a seventh gear slot, whether **Petrify** was cut on purpose, where **elemental resistance** lives, whether **the Inn** gets added to the six shops, and whether everything genuinely has to be a hexad — **World Modes** landed on three, maybe four.

The whole thing is in that checkpoint, verbatim, with every discrepancy flagged rather than smoothed. And Sections 15 and 16 of your original — the Act I Skills and Invocations references — I saw in the table of contents and never read. Those are still sitting there unopened.

"Three P's and three C's, matching the Heuristics split exactly. And the division isn't decorative — **the P-block is what the hero persistently has, the C-block is what happens in play.**"

## 5 The Heroics Metasystem
### 25 Heroic Prowess System
### 26 Heroic Provision System
### 27 Heroic Passage System
### 28 Heroic Conjuration System
### 29 Heroic Contention System
### 30 Heroic Condition System

**Prowess** — Stats, Combat Classes, Craft Classes, Derived Stats, and the XP/skill/spell progression curves. Everything the character *is*, numerically and by role, plus how it grows. The class stat-boost percentages live here too, which is where that 12%/4%-versus-9%/3% conflict needs settling.

**Provision** — Weapons, Instruments, Gear, Inventory categories, Drops and Currency. What the hero is furnished with. The 36 affixes and five tiers are sub-structure of Gear rather than their own hexad, and the Eternal Seeker signature superweapons sit here as an exception tier.

**Passage** — World Modes, movement gating (dungeon foot-only, town foot-or-mount, world-map everything), Weather/Biome/Day-Period, Shops, and the Inn. Where you go and what governs getting there.

**Conjuration** — Elements, Elemental Pairings, the Elemental Wheel, Tutelary Gods, and the 36-constellation Summon System. Everything you call on rather than carry.

**Contention** — Battle Actions, Battle Positions, Battle Participants, Beast Types. The encounter engine proper.

**Condition** — all four status hexads: generic negative, generic positive, classic negative, classic positive. States a body can be in.

---

**Now here's the part that made this worth doing.** Sort the twenty-six confirmed hexads into those six and count:

| System | Hexads |
|---|---|
| Prowess | 5 |
| Provision | 5 |
| Conjuration | 5 |
| Contention | 4 |
| Condition | 4 |
| Passage | 3 |

Twenty-six. Which is **exactly** where the checkpoint left the count — 26 hexads, 156 of 180, four hexads short.

If the thirty distribute evenly at five per system — and six systems of five is how you'd get thirty — then the frame doesn't just sort the material. **It tells you where the four missing hexads live.** One in Contention, one in Condition, two in Passage. Not scattered anywhere in a 180-slot field. Four specific holes with addresses.

And Passage being two short is the least surprising thing in the world, since it's the system holding **Weather, Biome, and Day-Period** — your own Triad 6, floated with zero members named — and **the Inn**, which you correctly identified as a real gap that isn't one of the six Shops.

**Two things this frame also clarifies.** Magic Hands & Functions and Elemental Pairings now sit side by side in Conjuration, which makes the redundancy I flagged much harder to ignore — if they merge, Conjuration is a clean 5. And Class-Changing Places dissolving into Shops means Passage was never over-full, it was under-full the whole time.

The two I'm least confident in are **Contention** (it's the right meaning but a slightly stiff word — Combat, Conflict, or Clash would all do the job) and **Passage** (which carries movement well but Shops and the Inn sit in it a bit awkwardly; Precinct or Province would hold the places better and the travel worse).
